href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1239400231">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}@media(prefers-color-scheme:dark){html.skin-theme-clientpref-os .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}}@media print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Geology_sidebar" title="Template:Geology sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Geology_sidebar" title="Template talk:Geology sidebar"><abbr template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Geology</b> is a branch of <a href="/wiki/Natural_science" title="Natural science">natural science</a> concerned with the Earth and other <a href="/wiki/Astronomical_bodies" class="mw-redirect" title="Astronomical bodies">astronomical bodies</a>, the rocks of which they are composed, and the processes by which they change over time.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Geology&#95;Definition&#95;1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geology_Definition-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The name comes from <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a> <i> </i><span lang="grc"><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%B3%E1%BF%86#Ancient_Greek" class="extiw" title="wikt:γῆ">γῆ</a></span><i> (<span title="Ancient Greek transliteration" lang="grc-Latn"><i>gê</i></span>)</i> <span class="gloss-quot">'</span><span class="gloss-text">earth</span><span class="gloss-quot">'</span> and <i> </i><span lang="grc"><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/-#Ancient_Greek" class="extiw" title="wikt:-">λoγία</a></span><i> (<span title="Ancient Greek transliteration" lang="grc-Latn"><i><a href="/wiki/-logy" title="-logy">-logía</a></i></span>)</i> <span class="gloss-quot">'</span><span class="gloss-text">study of, discourse</span><span class="gloss-quot">'</span>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-OnlineEtDict&#95;2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OnlineEtDict-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-LSJ&#95;3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LSJ-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Modern geology significantly overlaps all other <a href="/wiki/Earth_science" title="Earth science">Earth sciences</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Hydrology" title="Hydrology">hydrology</a>. It is integrated with <a href="/wiki/Earth_system_science" title="Earth system science">Earth system science</a> and <a href="/wiki/Planetary_science" title="Planetary science">planetary science</a>.
</p><p>Geology describes the <a href="/wiki/Structure_of_the_Earth" class="mw-redirect" title="Structure of the Earth">structure of the Earth</a> on and beneath its surface and the processes that have shaped that structure. <a href="/wiki/Geologists" class="mw-redirect" title="Geologists">Geologists</a> study the mineralogical composition of rocks in order to get insight into their history of formation. Geology determines the <a href="/wiki/Relative_ages" class="mw-redirect" title="Relative ages">relative ages</a> of rocks found at a given location; <a href="/wiki/Geochemistry" title="Geochemistry">geochemistry</a> (a branch of geology) determines their <a href="/wiki/Geochronology" title="Geochronology">absolute ages</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> By combining various petrological, crystallographic, and paleontological tools, <a href="/wiki/Geologist" title="Geologist">geologists</a> are able to chronicle the geological <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Earth" class="mw-redirect" title="History of the Earth">history of the Earth</a> as a whole. One aspect is to demonstrate the <a href="/wiki/Age_of_the_Earth" class="mw-redirect" title="Age of the Earth">age of the Earth</a>. Geology provides evidence for <a href="/wiki/Plate_tectonics" title="Plate tectonics">plate tectonics</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Evolutionary_history_of_life" class="mw-redirect" title="Evolutionary history of life">evolutionary history of life</a>, and the Earth's <a href="/wiki/Past_climates" class="mw-redirect" title="Past climates">past climates</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Geologist" title="Geologist">Geologists</a> broadly study the properties and processes of Earth and other terrestrial planets. Geologists use a wide variety of methods to understand the Earth's structure and evolution, including <a href="/wiki/Fieldwork" class="mw-redirect" title="Fieldwork">fieldwork</a>, <a href="/wiki/Petrology" title="Petrology">rock description</a>, <a href="/wiki/Geophysical_survey" title="Geophysical survey">geophysical techniques</a>, <a href="/wiki/Geochemistry" title="Geochemistry">chemical analysis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Physical_experiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Physical experiment">physical experiments</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Numerical_modelling" class="mw-redirect" title="Numerical modelling">numerical modelling</a>. In practical terms, geology is important for <a href="/wiki/Mining" title="Mining">mineral</a> and <a href="/wiki/Petroleum_geology" title="Petroleum geology">hydrocarbon</a> exploration and exploitation, evaluating <a href="/wiki/Water_resources" title="Water resources">water resources</a>, understanding <a href="/wiki/Natural_hazard" class="mw-redirect" title="Natural hazard">natural hazards</a>, remediating <a href="/wiki/Environmental_Geology" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental Geology">environmental</a> problems, and providing insights into past <a href="/wiki/Climate_variability_and_change" title="Climate variability and change">climate change</a>. Geology is a major <a href="/wiki/Academic_discipline" title="Academic discipline">academic discipline</a>, and it is central to <a href="/wiki/Geological_engineering" title="Geological engineering">geological engineering</a> and plays an important role in <a href="/wiki/Geotechnical_engineering" title="Geotechnical engineering">geotechnical engineering</a>.
</p>

href="/wiki/File:Or_Venezuela.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Or_Venezuela.jpg/250px-Or_Venezuela.jpg" decoding="async" width="132" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:172px;max-width:172px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:170px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Quartz%2C_Tibet.jpg/500px-Quartz%2C_Tibet.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1592" data-file-height="1596" /></a></span></div></div><div class="tsingle" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Petrified_forest_log_1_md.jpg/120px-Petrified_forest_log_1_md.jpg" decoding="async" width="114" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Petrified_forest_log_1_md.jpg/250px-Petrified_forest_log_1_md.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="821" <a href="/wiki/Badlands_National_Park" title="Badlands National Park">Badlands National Park</a>, South Dakota; <a href="/wiki/Native_metal" title="Native metal">native</a> <a href="/wiki/Gold#Occurrence" title="Gold">gold</a> from <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuela</a>; <a href="/wiki/Petrified" class="mw-redirect" title="Petrified">petrified</a> log in <a href="/wiki/Petrified_Forest_National_Park" title="Petrified Forest National Park">Petrified Forest National Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arizona" title="Arizona">Arizona</a>, US; <a href="/wiki/Quartz" title="Quartz">quartz</a> from <a href="/wiki/Tibet" title="Tibet">Tibet</a>; metamorphic rock, Nunavut, Canada</div></div></div></div>
<p>The majority of geological data comes from research on solid Earth materials. Meteorites and other extraterrestrial natural materials are also studied by geological methods.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Minerals">Minerals</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Minerals"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mineral" title="Mineral">Mineral</a></div>
<p>Minerals are naturally occurring <a href="/wiki/Chemical_element" title="Chemical element">elements</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chemical_compound" title="Chemical compound">compounds</a> with a definite homogeneous chemical composition and an ordered atomic arrangement. Amorphous substances that resemble a mineral are sometimes referred to as <a href="/wiki/Mineraloid" title="Mineraloid">mineraloids</a>, although there are exceptions such as <a href="/wiki/Georgeite" title="Georgeite">georgeite</a> and <a href="/wiki/Autunite" title="Autunite">autunite</a>. Some amorphous substances formed by geological processes are considered minerals if the original substance was a mineral before <a href="/wiki/Metamictisation" title="Metamictisation">metamictisation</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Each mineral has distinct physical properties, and there are many tests to determine each of them. Minerals are often identified through these tests. The specimens can be tested for:<sup id="cite&#95;ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Color: Minerals are grouped by their color. Mostly diagnostic but impurities can change a mineral's color.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Streak_(mineralogy)" title="Streak (mineralogy)">Streak</a>: Performed by scratching the sample on a <a href="/wiki/Porcelain" title="Porcelain">porcelain</a> plate. The color of the streak can help identify the mineral.</li>
<li>Hardness: The resistance of a mineral to scratching or indentation.</li>
<li>Breakage pattern: A mineral can either show fracture or <a href="/wiki/Cleavage_(crystal)" title="Cleavage (crystal)">cleavage</a>, the former being breakage of uneven surfaces, and the latter a breakage along closely spaced parallel planes.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lustre_(mineralogy)" title="Lustre (mineralogy)">Luster</a>: Quality of light reflected from the surface of a mineral. Examples are metallic, pearly, waxy, dull.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Specific_gravity" class="mw-redirect" title="Specific gravity">Specific gravity</a>: the weight of a specific volume of a mineral.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Effervescence" title="Effervescence">Effervescence</a>: Involves dripping <a href="/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid" title="Hydrochloric acid">hydrochloric acid</a> on the mineral to test for fizzing.</li>
<li>Magnetism: Involves using a magnet to test for <a href="/wiki/Magnetism" title="Magnetism">magnetism</a>.</li>
<li>Taste: Minerals can have a distinctive taste such as <a href="/wiki/Halite" title="Halite">halite</a> (which tastes like <a href="/wiki/Table_salt" class="mw-redirect" title="Table salt">table salt</a>).</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Rock">Rock</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Rock"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cycle_of_rocks_2.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Cycle_of_rocks_2.png/500px-Cycle_of_rocks_2.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="268" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Cycle_of_rocks_2.png/960px-Cycle_of_rocks_2.png 1.5x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="2300" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Rock_cycle" title="Rock cycle">rock cycle</a> shows the relationship between <a href="/wiki/Igneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Igneous">igneous</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sedimentary" class="mw-redirect" title="Sedimentary">sedimentary</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Metamorphic" class="mw-redirect" title="Metamorphic">metamorphic</a> <a href="/wiki/Rock_(geology)" title="Rock (geology)">rocks</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Rock_(geology)" title="Rock (geology)">Rock (geology)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rock_cycle" title="Rock cycle">Rock cycle</a></div>
<p>A rock is any naturally occurring solid mass or aggregate of minerals or <a href="/wiki/Mineraloid" title="Mineraloid">mineraloids</a>. Most research in geology is associated with the study of rocks, as they provide the primary record of the majority of the geological history of the Earth. There are three major types of rock: <a href="/wiki/Igneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Igneous">igneous</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sedimentary" class="mw-redirect" title="Sedimentary">sedimentary</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Metamorphic" class="mw-redirect" title="Metamorphic">metamorphic</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Rock_cycle" title="Rock cycle">rock cycle</a>
illustrates the relationships among them (see diagram).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>When a rock <a href="/wiki/Solidifies" class="mw-redirect" title="Solidifies">solidifies</a> or <a href="/wiki/Crystallizes" class="mw-redirect" title="Crystallizes">crystallizes</a> from melt (<a href="/wiki/Magma" title="Magma">magma</a> or <a href="/wiki/Lava" title="Lava">lava</a>), it is an <a href="/wiki/Igneous_rock" title="Igneous rock">igneous rock</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-USGS&#95;Igneous&#95;8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USGS_Igneous-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The active flow of molten rock is closely studied in <a href="/wiki/Volcanology" title="Volcanology">volcanology</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Igneous_petrology" title="Igneous petrology">igneous petrology</a> aims to determine the history of <a href="/wiki/Igneous_rock" title="Igneous rock">igneous rocks</a> from their original molten source to their final crystallization.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Rocks can be <a href="/wiki/Weathering" title="Weathering">weathered</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eroded" class="mw-redirect" title="Eroded">eroded</a>, then <a href="/wiki/Deposition_(geology)" title="Deposition (geology)">redeposited</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lithified" class="mw-redirect" title="Lithified">lithified</a> into a sedimentary rock. Sedimentary rocks are mainly divided into four categories: sandstone, shale, carbonate, and evaporite. This group of classifications focuses partly on the size of sedimentary particles (sandstone and shale), and partly on mineralogy and formation processes (carbonation and evaporation).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Igneous and sedimentary rocks can then be turned into <a href="/wiki/Metamorphic_rock" title="Metamorphic rock">metamorphic rocks</a> by heat and pressure that change its <a href="/wiki/Mineral" title="Mineral">mineral</a> content, resulting in a <a href="/wiki/Fabric_(geology)" title="Fabric (geology)">characteristic fabric</a>. All three types may melt again, and when this happens, new magma is formed, from which an igneous rock may once again solidify. Organic matter, such as coal, bitumen, oil, and natural gas, is linked mainly to organic-rich sedimentary rocks.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>To study all three types of rock, geologists evaluate the minerals of which they are composed and their other physical properties, such as <a href="/wiki/Texture_(geology)" title="Texture (geology)">texture</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fabric_(geology)" title="Fabric (geology)">fabric</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Unlithified_material">Unlithified material</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Unlithified material"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Geologists study unlithified materials (referred to as <i><a href="/wiki/Superficial_deposits" title="Superficial deposits">superficial deposits</a></i>) that lie above the <a href="/wiki/Bedrock" title="Bedrock">bedrock</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This study is often known as <a href="/wiki/Quaternary_geology" title="Quaternary geology">Quaternary geology</a>, after the <a href="/wiki/Quaternary_period" class="mw-redirect" title="Quaternary period">Quaternary period</a> of geologic history, which is the most recent period of geologic time.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Whole-Earth_structure">Whole-Earth structure</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Whole-Earth structure"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Plate_tectonics">Plate tectonics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Plate tectonics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Plate_tectonics" title="Plate tectonics">Plate tectonics</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Plates_tect2_en.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Plates_tect2_en.svg/500px-Plates_tect2_en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="340" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Plates_tect2_en.svg/960px-Plates_tect2_en.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="4167" data-file-height="2844" /></a><figcaption>The major <a href="/wiki/Tectonic_plates" class="mw-redirect" title="Tectonic plates">tectonic plates</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Earth" title="Earth">Earth</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the 1960s, it was discovered that the Earth's <a href="/wiki/Lithosphere" title="Lithosphere">lithosphere</a>, which includes the <a href="/wiki/Crust_(geology)" title="Crust (geology)">crust</a> and rigid uppermost portion of the <a href="/wiki/Upper_mantle" title="Upper mantle">upper mantle</a>, is separated into <a href="/wiki/Tectonic_plate" class="mw-redirect" title="Tectonic plate">tectonic plates</a> that move across the <a href="/wiki/Plasticity_(physics)" title="Plasticity (physics)">plastically</a> deforming, solid, upper mantle, which is called the <a href="/wiki/Asthenosphere" title="Asthenosphere">asthenosphere</a>. This theory is supported by several types of observations, including seafloor spreading<sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-TDE&#95;discovery&#95;16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TDE_discovery-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the global distribution of mountain terrain and seismicity.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>There is an intimate coupling between the movement of the plates on the surface and the <a href="/wiki/Mantle_convection" title="Mantle convection">convection of the mantle</a> (that is, the <a href="/wiki/Heat" title="Heat">heat</a> transfer caused by the slow movement of ductile mantle rock). Thus, oceanic parts of plates and the adjoining mantle <a href="/wiki/Convection_currents" class="mw-redirect" title="Convection currents">convection currents</a> always move in the same direction – because the oceanic lithosphere is actually the rigid upper thermal <a href="/wiki/Boundary_layer" title="Boundary layer">boundary layer</a> of the convecting mantle. This coupling between rigid plates moving on the surface of the Earth and the convecting <a href="/wiki/Mantle_(geology)" title="Mantle (geology)">mantle</a> is called plate <a href="/wiki/Tectonics" title="Tectonics">tectonics</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The development of plate tectonics has provided a physical basis for many observations of the solid <a href="/wiki/Earth" title="Earth">Earth</a>. Long linear regions of geological features are explained as plate boundaries:<sup id="cite&#95;ref-TDE&#95;plates&#95;19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TDE_plates-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:left;" class=""></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Active_Margin.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Active_Margin.svg/250px-Active_Margin.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Active_Margin.svg/500px-Active_Margin.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="287" /></a><figcaption>Oceanic-continental convergence resulting in <a href="/wiki/Subduction" title="Subduction">subduction</a> and <a href="/wiki/Volcanic_arc" title="Volcanic arc">volcanic arcs</a> illustrates one effect of <a href="/wiki/Plate_tectonics" title="Plate tectonics">plate tectonics</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mid-ocean_ridge" title="Mid-ocean ridge">Mid-ocean ridges</a>, high regions on the seafloor where <a href="/wiki/Hydrothermal_vent" title="Hydrothermal vent">hydrothermal vents</a> and volcanoes exist, are seen as <a href="/wiki/Divergent_boundary" title="Divergent boundary">divergent boundaries</a>, where two plates move apart.</li>
<li>Arcs of volcanoes and earthquakes are theorized as <a href="/wiki/Convergent_boundaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Convergent boundaries">convergent boundaries</a>, where one plate <a href="/wiki/Subducts" class="mw-redirect" title="Subducts">subducts</a>, or moves, under another.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Transform_boundary" class="mw-redirect" title="Transform boundary">Transform boundaries</a>, such as the <a href="/wiki/San_Andreas_Fault" title="San Andreas Fault">San Andreas Fault</a> system, are where plates slide horizontally past each other.</li></ul>
<p>Plate tectonics has provided a mechanism for <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Wegener" title="Alfred Wegener">Alfred Wegener</a>'s theory of <a href="/wiki/Continental_drift" title="Continental drift">continental drift</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> in which the <a href="/wiki/Continents" class="mw-redirect" title="Continents">continents</a> move across the surface of the Earth over geological time. They provided a driving force for crustal deformation, and a new setting for the observations of structural geology. The power of the theory of plate tectonics lies in its ability to combine all of these observations into a single theory of how the lithosphere moves over the convecting mantle, forming a "grand unifying theory of geology".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Earth_structure">Earth structure</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Earth structure"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Internal_structure_of_Earth" title="Internal structure of Earth">Internal structure of Earth</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jordens_inre-numbers.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Jordens_inre-numbers.svg/250px-Jordens_inre-numbers.svg.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Jordens_inre-numbers.svg/330px-Jordens_inre-numbers.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Jordens_inre-numbers.svg/500px-Jordens_inre-numbers.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Earth" title="Earth">Earth</a>'s layered structure. (1) inner core; (2) outer core; (3) lower mantle; (4) upper mantle; (5) lithosphere; (6) crust (uppermost part of the lithosphere)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Advances in <a href="/wiki/Seismology" title="Seismology">seismology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Computer_modeling" class="mw-redirect" title="Computer modeling">computer modeling</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mineralogy" title="Mineralogy">mineralogy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Crystallography" title="Crystallography">crystallography</a> at high temperatures and pressures give insights into the internal composition and structure of the Earth.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Earthquake_wave_paths.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Earthquake_wave_paths.svg/330px-Earthquake_wave_paths.svg.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Earthquake_wave_paths.svg/500px-Earthquake_wave_paths.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Earthquake_wave_paths.svg/960px-Earthquake_wave_paths.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="250" /></a><figcaption>Typical wave paths from earthquakes like these gave early seismologists insights into the layered structure of the Earth.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Seismologists can use the arrival times of <a href="/wiki/Seismic_wave" title="Seismic wave">seismic waves</a> to image the interior of the Earth. Early advances in this field showed the existence of a liquid <a href="/wiki/Outer_core" class="mw-redirect" title="Outer core">outer core</a> (where <a href="/wiki/S-wave" class="mw-redirect" title="S-wave">shear waves</a> were not able to propagate) and a dense solid <a href="/wiki/Inner_core" class="mw-redirect" title="Inner core">inner core</a>. These advances led to the development of a layered model of the Earth, with a <a href="/wiki/Lithosphere" title="Lithosphere">lithosphere</a> (including crust) on top, the <a href="/wiki/Earth%27s_mantle" title="Earth&#39;s mantle">mantle</a> below (separated within itself by <a href="/wiki/Seismic_tomography" title="Seismic tomography">seismic discontinuities</a> at 410 and 660 kilometers), and the outer core and inner core below that.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Fichtner&#95;2010&#95;24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fichtner_2010-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Starting in the 1970s, seismologists have been able to use new techniques such as seismic <a href="/w/index.php?title=Full-waveform_inversion&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Full-waveform inversion (page does not exist)">full-waveform inversion</a> to create detailed images of wave speeds inside the earth in the same way a doctor images a body in a <a href="/wiki/CT_scan" title="CT scan">CT scan</a>. These images have led to a much more detailed view of the interior of the Earth, and have replaced the simplified layered model with a much more dynamic model.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Fichtner&#95;2010&#95;24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fichtner_2010-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Mineralogists have been able to use the pressure and temperature data from the seismic and modeling studies alongside knowledge of the elemental composition of the Earth to reproduce these conditions in experimental settings and measure changes within the crystal structure.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These studies explain the chemical changes associated with the major seismic discontinuities in the mantle<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and show the crystallographic structures expected in the inner core of the Earth.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Geological_time">Geological time</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Geological time"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geologic_time_scale" title="Geologic time scale">Geologic time scale</a></div>
<p>The geological time scale encompasses the history of the Earth.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It is bracketed at the earliest by the dates of the first <a href="/wiki/Solar_System" title="Solar System">Solar System</a> material at 4.567 <a href="/wiki/Gigaannum" class="mw-redirect" title="Gigaannum">Ga</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.567&#95;31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.567-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> (or 4.567 billion years ago) and the formation of the Earth at
4.54 Ga<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.54&#95;32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.54-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.54&#95;book&#95;33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.54_book-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
(4.54 billion years), which is the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Hadean_eon" class="mw-redirect" title="Hadean eon">Hadean eon</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;a division of geological time. At the later end of the scale, it is marked by the present day (in the <a href="/wiki/Holocene_epoch" class="mw-redirect" title="Holocene epoch">Holocene epoch</a>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Timescale_of_the_Earth">Timescale of the Earth</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Timescale of the Earth"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geological_history_of_Earth" title="Geological history of Earth">Geological history of Earth</a></div>
<p>The following five timelines show the geologic time scale to scale. The first shows the entire time from the formation of Earth to the present, but this gives little space for the most recent eon. The second timeline shows an expanded view of the most recent eon. In a similar way, the most recent era is expanded in the third timeline, the most recent period is expanded in the fourth timeline, and the most recent epoch is expanded in the fifth timeline.
</p>
<div style="text-align:center;">
