<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Important_milestones_on_Earth">Important milestones on Earth</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Important milestones on Earth"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Geologic_Clock_with_events_and_periods.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Geologic_Clock_with_events_and_periods.svg/500px-Geologic_Clock_with_events_and_periods.svg.png" decoding="async" width="340" height="326" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Geologic_Clock_with_events_and_periods.svg/960px-Geologic_Clock_with_events_and_periods.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="491" /></a><figcaption>Geological time in a diagram called a <a href="/wiki/Geological_clock" class="mw-redirect" title="Geological clock">geological clock</a>, showing the relative lengths of the <a href="/wiki/Geologic_eon" class="mw-redirect" title="Geologic eon">eons</a> and <a href="/wiki/Era_(geology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Era (geology)">eras</a> of the Earth's history</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li>4.567 <a href="/wiki/Ga_(unit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ga (unit)">Ga</a> (gigaannum: billion years ago): <a href="/wiki/Solar_system_formation" class="mw-redirect" title="Solar system formation">Solar system formation</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.567&#95;31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.567-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>4.54 Ga: <a href="/wiki/Accretion_(astrophysics)" title="Accretion (astrophysics)">Accretion, or formation</a>, of Earth<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.54&#95;32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.54-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-4.54&#95;book&#95;33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4.54_book-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>4.5 Ga: Proposed <a href="/wiki/Giant-impact_hypothesis" title="Giant-impact hypothesis">Moon-forming impact</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>c. 4 Ga: End of <a href="/wiki/Late_Heavy_Bombardment" title="Late Heavy Bombardment">Late Heavy Bombardment</a>, the first life</li>
<li>c. 3.5 Ga: Start of <a href="/wiki/Photosynthesis" title="Photosynthesis">photosynthesis</a></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7017100984320000000♠"></span>3.2–2.3&#160;Ga</span>: Transition of crust from <a href="/wiki/Lid_tectonics" title="Lid tectonics">stagnant lid</a> to plate tectonics<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>c. 2.3 Ga: Oxygenated <a href="/wiki/Atmosphere" title="Atmosphere">atmosphere</a>, first <a href="/wiki/Snowball_Earth" title="Snowball Earth">snowball Earth</a></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7016568036800000000♠"></span>1.8–1.5&#160;Ga</span>: <a href="/wiki/Columbia_(supercontinent)" title="Columbia (supercontinent)">Columbia</a> <a href="/wiki/Supercontinent" title="Supercontinent">supercontinent</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Vázquez&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2010&#95;37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vázquez_et_al_2010-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7016347133600000000♠"></span>1,100–750&#160;<a href="/wiki/Megaannum" class="mw-redirect" title="Megaannum">Ma</a></span> (megaannum: million years ago): <a href="/wiki/Rodinia" title="Rodinia">Rodinia</a> supercontinent<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Vázquez&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2010&#95;37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vázquez_et_al_2010-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7016230370480000000♠"></span>730–635&#160;Ma</span>: second snowball Earth</li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7016205124400000000♠"></span>650–540&#160;Ma</span>: <a href="/wiki/Pannotia" title="Pannotia">Pannotia</a> supercontinent<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Vázquez&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2010&#95;37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vázquez_et_al_2010-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7016170726616000000♠"></span>541<span style="margin-left:0.225em;margin-right:0.225em;">±</span>0.3&#160;Ma</span>: <a href="/wiki/Cambrian_explosion" title="Cambrian explosion">Cambrian explosion</a> – vast multiplication of hard-bodied life; first abundant <a href="/wiki/Fossil" title="Fossil">fossils</a>; start of the <a href="/wiki/Paleozoic" title="Paleozoic">Paleozoic</a></li>
<li>c. 380 Ma: First <a href="/wiki/Vertebrate" title="Vertebrate">vertebrate</a> land animals</li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7015946728000000000♠"></span>300–180&#160;Ma</span>: <a href="/wiki/Pangaea" title="Pangaea">Pangaea</a> supercontinent<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Vázquez&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2010&#95;37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vázquez_et_al_2010-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>250 Ma: <a href="/wiki/Permian-Triassic_extinction" class="mw-redirect" title="Permian-Triassic extinction">Permian-Triassic extinction</a> – 90% of all land animals die; end of Paleozoic and beginning of <a href="/wiki/Mesozoic" title="Mesozoic">Mesozoic</a></li>
<li>66 Ma: <a href="/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction" class="mw-redirect" title="Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction">Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction</a> – <a href="/wiki/Dinosaur" title="Dinosaur">Dinosaurs</a> die; end of Mesozoic and beginning of <a href="/wiki/Cenozoic" title="Cenozoic">Cenozoic</a></li>
<li><span class="nowrap"><span data-sort-value="7015142009200000000♠"></span>45–35&#160;Ma</span>: <a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himalayas</a> mountain range forms<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>c. 7 Ma: First <a href="/wiki/Hominin" class="mw-redirect" title="Hominin">hominins</a> appear</li>
<li>3.9 Ma: First <a href="/wiki/Australopithecus" title="Australopithecus">Australopithecus</a>, direct ancestor to modern <a href="/wiki/Homo_sapiens" class="mw-redirect" title="Homo sapiens">Homo sapiens</a>, appear</li>
<li>200 <a href="/wiki/Ma_(unit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ma (unit)">ka</a> (kiloannum: thousand years ago): First modern Homo sapiens appear in East Africa</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Timescale_of_the_Moon">Timescale of the Moon</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Timescale of the Moon"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Lunar_geologic_timescale" title="Lunar geologic timescale">Lunar geologic timescale</a></div>
<div class="timeline-wrapper"><map name="timeline_rqgvwx9mhj95lj57eva0a98t8ypxloi"><area href="/wiki/Early_Imbrian" coords="115,34,179,55" title="Early Imbrian" alt="Early Imbrian" /><area href="/wiki/Late_Imbrian" coords="186,34,244,55" title="Late Imbrian" alt="Late Imbrian" /><area href="/wiki/Pre%2DNectarian" coords="53,9,161,30" title="Pre-Nectarian" alt="Pre-Nectarian" /><area href="/wiki/Nectarian" coords="139,9,181,30" title="Nectarian" alt="Nectarian" /><area href="/wiki/Eratosthenian" coords="360,9,468,30" title="Eratosthenian" alt="Eratosthenian" /><area href="/wiki/Copernican_period" coords="605,9,697,30" title="Copernican period" alt="Copernican period" /></map><img usemap="#timeline_rqgvwx9mhj95lj57eva0a98t8ypxloi" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/timeline/rqgvwx9mhj95lj57eva0a98t8ypxloi.png" /></div>
<div class="center"><small>Millions of years before present</small></div>
<p>The epochs of lunar history are based on the chronology of <a href="/wiki/Impact_event" title="Impact event">impact events</a>, and they are named after defining major impacts. Hence, the Imbrian is named after the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Mare_Imbrium" title="Mare Imbrium">Mare Imbrium</a> basin. The ages of older <a href="/wiki/Lunar_basin" class="mw-redirect" title="Lunar basin">lunar basins</a> can be dated based on the strength of their intrinsic magnetic field, since the early <a href="/wiki/Magnetic_field_of_the_Moon" title="Magnetic field of the Moon">Moon had a magnetic field</a> that faded over time. The ages of craters can be estimated by morphological and stratigraphic classifications, with younger craters overlapping older impacts and generally showing less impact wear.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Timescale_of_Mars">Timescale of Mars</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Timescale of Mars"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geological_history_of_Mars" title="Geological history of Mars">Geological history of Mars</a></div>
<div class="timeline-wrapper"><map name="timeline_qet6aufoz6py1mu8mqq7qxqakgkq7df"><area href="/wiki/Pre%2DNoachian" coords="-1,9,101,30" title="Pre-Noachian" alt="Pre-Noachian" /><area href="/wiki/Noachian" coords="80,9,161,30" title="Noachian" alt="Noachian" /><area href="/wiki/Hesperian" coords="171,9,257,30" title="Hesperian" alt="Hesperian" /><area href="/wiki/Amazonian_(Mars)" coords="485,9,571,30" title="Amazonian (Mars)" alt="Amazonian (Mars)" /></map><img usemap="#timeline_qet6aufoz6py1mu8mqq7qxqakgkq7df" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/timeline/qet6aufoz6py1mu8mqq7qxqakgkq7df.png" /></div>
<div class="center"><small>Martian time periods (millions of years ago)</small></div>
<p>Epochs:
</p>
<div class="timeline-wrapper"><map name="timeline_l9mdbu2kk36h4dwzxwhwkvq47h8t0cb"></map><img usemap="#timeline_l9mdbu2kk36h4dwzxwhwkvq47h8t0cb" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/timeline/l9mdbu2kk36h4dwzxwhwkvq47h8t0cb.png" /></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Dating_methods">Dating methods</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Dating methods"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Relative_dating">Relative dating</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Relative dating"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Relative_dating" title="Relative dating">Relative dating</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cross-cutting_relations.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cross-cutting_relations.svg/500px-Cross-cutting_relations.svg.png" decoding="async" width="340" height="184" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cross-cutting_relations.svg/960px-Cross-cutting_relations.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="540" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Cross-cutting_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Cross-cutting relations">Cross-cutting relations</a> can be used to determine the relative ages of <a href="/wiki/Rock_strata" class="mw-redirect" title="Rock strata">rock strata</a> and other geological structures. Explanations: A – <a href="/wiki/Fold_(geology)" title="Fold (geology)">folded</a> rock strata cut by a <a href="/wiki/Thrust_fault" title="Thrust fault">thrust fault</a>; B – large <a href="/wiki/Intrusion" class="mw-redirect" title="Intrusion">intrusion</a> (cutting through A); C – <a href="/wiki/Erosion" title="Erosion">erosional</a> <a href="/wiki/Angular_unconformity" class="mw-redirect" title="Angular unconformity">angular unconformity</a> (cutting off A &amp; B) on which rock strata were deposited; D – <a href="/wiki/Dike_(geology)" title="Dike (geology)">volcanic dyke</a> (cutting through A, B &amp; C); E – even younger rock strata (overlying C &amp; D); F – <a href="/wiki/Normal_fault" class="mw-redirect" title="Normal fault">normal fault</a> (cutting through A, B, C &amp; E).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Methods for <a href="/wiki/Relative_dating" title="Relative dating">relative dating</a> were developed when geology first emerged as a <a href="/wiki/Natural_science" title="Natural science">natural science</a>. Geologists still use the following principles today as a means to provide information about geological history and the timing of geological events.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Jain&#95;2013&#95;40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jain_2013-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Principle_of_uniformitarianism" class="mw-redirect" title="Principle of uniformitarianism">principle of uniformitarianism</a></i> states that the geological processes observed in operation that modify the Earth's crust at present have worked in much the same way over geological time.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A fundamental principle of geology advanced by the 18th-century Scottish physician and geologist <a href="/wiki/James_Hutton" title="James Hutton">James Hutton</a> is that "the present is the key to the past." In Hutton's words: "the past history of our globe must be explained by what can be seen to be happening now."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Intrusion_(geology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Intrusion (geology)">principle of intrusive relationships</a></i> concerns crosscutting intrusions. In geology, when an <a href="/wiki/Igneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Igneous">igneous</a> intrusion cuts across a formation of <a href="/wiki/Sedimentary_rock" title="Sedimentary rock">sedimentary rock</a>, it can be determined that the igneous intrusion is younger than the sedimentary rock.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Different types of intrusions include stocks, <a href="/wiki/Laccolith" title="Laccolith">laccoliths</a>, <a href="/wiki/Batholith" title="Batholith">batholiths</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sill_(geology)" title="Sill (geology)">sills</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dike_(geology)" title="Dike (geology)">dikes</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hunt&#95;2005&#95;45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hunt_2005-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Principle_of_cross-cutting_relationships" class="mw-redirect" title="Principle of cross-cutting relationships">principle of cross-cutting relationships</a></i> pertains to the formation of <a href="/wiki/Fault_(geology)" title="Fault (geology)">faults</a> and the age of the sequences through which they cut. Faults are younger than the rocks they cut; accordingly, if a fault is found that penetrates some formations but not those on top of it, then the formations that were cut are older than the fault, and the ones that are not cut must be younger than the fault.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Finding the key bed in these situations may help determine whether the fault is a <a href="/wiki/Normal_fault" class="mw-redirect" title="Normal fault">normal fault</a> or a <a href="/wiki/Thrust_fault" title="Thrust fault">thrust fault</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-steno&#95;strat&#95;46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-steno_strat-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Principle_of_inclusions_and_components" class="mw-redirect" title="Principle of inclusions and components">principle of inclusions and components</a></i> states that, with sedimentary rocks, if inclusions (or <i><a href="/wiki/Clasts" class="mw-redirect" title="Clasts">clasts</a></i>) are found in a formation, then the inclusions must be older than the formation that contains them.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For example, in sedimentary rocks, it is common for gravel from an older formation to be ripped up and included in a newer layer. A similar situation with igneous rocks occurs when <a href="/wiki/Xenolith" title="Xenolith">xenoliths</a> are found. These foreign bodies are picked up as <a href="/wiki/Magma" title="Magma">magma</a> or lava flows, and are incorporated, later to cool in the matrix. As a result, xenoliths are older than the rock that contains them.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SEUtahStrat.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/SEUtahStrat.JPG/500px-SEUtahStrat.JPG" decoding="async" width="340" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/SEUtahStrat.JPG/960px-SEUtahStrat.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="3161" data-file-height="1794" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Permian" title="Permian">Permian</a> through <a href="/wiki/Jurassic" title="Jurassic">Jurassic</a> stratigraphy of the <a href="/wiki/Colorado_Plateau" title="Colorado Plateau">Colorado Plateau</a> area of southeastern <a href="/wiki/Utah" title="Utah">Utah</a> is an example of both original horizontality and the law of superposition. These strata make up much of the famous prominent rock formations in widely spaced protected areas such as <a href="/wiki/Capitol_Reef_National_Park" title="Capitol Reef National Park">Capitol Reef National Park</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canyonlands_National_Park" title="Canyonlands National Park">Canyonlands National Park</a>. From top to bottom: Rounded tan domes of the <a href="/wiki/Navajo_Sandstone" title="Navajo Sandstone">Navajo Sandstone</a>; layered red <a href="/wiki/Kayenta_Formation" title="Kayenta Formation">Kayenta Formation</a>; cliff-forming, vertically jointed, red <a href="/wiki/Wingate_Sandstone" title="Wingate Sandstone">Wingate Sandstone</a>; slope-forming, purplish <a href="/wiki/Chinle_Formation" title="Chinle Formation">Chinle Formation</a>; layered, lighter-red <a href="/wiki/Moenkopi_Formation" title="Moenkopi Formation">Moenkopi Formation</a>; and white, layered <a href="/wiki/Cutler_Formation" title="Cutler Formation">Cutler Formation</a> sandstone. Picture from <a href="/wiki/Glen_Canyon_National_Recreation_Area" title="Glen Canyon National Recreation Area">Glen Canyon National Recreation Area</a>, Utah.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Principle_of_original_horizontality" title="Principle of original horizontality">principle of original horizontality</a></i> states that the deposition of sediments occurs as essentially horizontal beds.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Observation of modern marine and non-marine sediments in a wide variety of environments supports this generalization (although <a href="/wiki/Cross-bedding" title="Cross-bedding">cross-bedding</a> is inclined, the overall orientation of cross-bedded units is horizontal).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-steno&#95;strat&#95;46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-steno_strat-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Law_of_superposition" title="Law of superposition">principle of superposition</a></i> states that a sedimentary rock layer in a <a href="/wiki/Tectonically" class="mw-redirect" title="Tectonically">tectonically</a> undisturbed sequence is younger than the one beneath it and older than the one above it. Logically a younger layer cannot slip beneath a layer previously deposited.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This principle allows sedimentary layers to be viewed as a form of the vertical timeline, a partial or complete record of the time elapsed from deposition of the lowest layer to deposition of the highest bed.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-steno&#95;strat&#95;46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-steno_strat-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Principle_of_faunal_succession" title="Principle of faunal succession">principle of faunal succession</a></i> is based on the appearance of fossils in sedimentary rocks. As organisms exist during the same period throughout the world, their presence or (sometimes) absence provides a relative age of the formations where they appear.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-West&#95;Shakoor&#95;2018&#95;42-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-West_Shakoor_2018-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Based on principles that William Smith laid out almost a hundred years before the publication of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Darwin" title="Charles Darwin">Charles Darwin</a>'s theory of <a href="/wiki/Evolution" title="Evolution">evolution</a>, the principles of succession developed independently of evolutionary thought. The principle becomes quite complex, however, given the uncertainties of fossilization, localization of fossil types due to lateral changes in habitat (<a href="/wiki/Facies" title="Facies">facies</a> change in sedimentary strata), and that not all fossils formed globally at the same time.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Absolute_dating">Absolute dating</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Absolute dating"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Absolute_dating" title="Absolute dating">Absolute dating</a>, <a href="/wiki/Radiometric_dating" title="Radiometric dating">radiometric dating</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Geochronology" title="Geochronology">geochronology</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg/250px-Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="342" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg/330px-Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg/500px-Zircon-tuc1001b.jpg 2x" data-file-width="999" data-file-height="1800" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Mineral" title="Mineral">mineral</a> <a href="/wiki/Zircon" title="Zircon">zircon</a> is often used in <a href="/wiki/Radiometric_dating" title="Radiometric dating">radiometric dating</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Geologists use methods to determine the absolute age of rock samples and geological events. These may be used in conjunction with relative dating methods or to calibrate relative methods.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>At the beginning of the 20th century, advancement in geological science was facilitated by the ability to obtain accurate absolute dates to geological events using <a href="/wiki/Radioactive_isotope" class="mw-redirect" title="Radioactive isotope">radioactive isotopes</a> and other methods. This changed the understanding of geological time. Previously, geologists could only use fossils and stratigraphic correlation to date sections of rock relative to one another. With isotopic dates, it became possible to assign <a href="/wiki/Absolute_ages" class="mw-redirect" title="Absolute ages">absolute ages</a> to rock units, and these absolute dates could be applied to fossil sequences in which there was datable material, converting the old relative ages into new absolute ages.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>For many geological applications, <a href="/wiki/Isotope_ratio" class="mw-redirect" title="Isotope ratio">isotope ratios</a> of radioactive elements are measured in minerals that give the amount of time that has passed since a rock passed through its particular <a href="/wiki/Closure_temperature" title="Closure temperature">closure temperature</a>: the point at which different radiometric isotopes stop <a href="/wiki/Atomic_diffusion" title="Atomic diffusion">diffusing</a> into and out of the <a href="/wiki/Crystal_lattice" class="mw-redirect" title="Crystal lattice">crystal lattice</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These are used in <a href="/wiki/Geochronologic" class="mw-redirect" title="Geochronologic">geochronologic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thermochronology" title="Thermochronology">thermochronologic</a> studies. The most suitable isotope systems for this purpose include <a href="/wiki/Uranium%E2%80%93lead_dating" title="Uranium–lead dating">uranium–lead</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rubidium%E2%80%93strontium_dating" title="Rubidium–strontium dating">rubidium–strontium</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Potassium%E2%80%93argon_dating" class="mw-redirect" title="Potassium–argon dating">potassium–argon</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Nance&#95;Murphy&#95;2016&#95;54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nance_Murphy_2016-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Uranium%E2%80%93thorium_dating" title="Uranium–thorium dating">Uranium–thorium dating</a> is used for dating calcium-carbonate.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Dating of <a href="/wiki/Lava" title="Lava">lava</a> and <a href="/wiki/Volcanic_ash" title="Volcanic ash">volcanic ash</a> layers found within a stratigraphic sequence can provide absolute age data for sedimentary rock units that do not contain radioactive isotopes and calibrate relative dating techniques.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These methods can be used to determine ages of <a href="/wiki/Pluton" class="mw-redirect" title="Pluton">pluton</a> emplacement. Fractionation of the <a href="/wiki/Lanthanide_series" class="mw-redirect" title="Lanthanide series">lanthanide series</a> elements is used to compute ages since rocks were removed from the mantle.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Other methods are used for more recent events. <a href="/wiki/Optically_stimulated_luminescence" title="Optically stimulated luminescence">Optically stimulated luminescence</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cosmogenic_isotope#Natural" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosmogenic isotope">cosmogenic radionuclide</a> dating are used to date surfaces and/or erosion rates.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dendrochronology" title="Dendrochronology">Dendrochronology</a> can be used for the dating of landscapes.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Radiocarbon_dating" title="Radiocarbon dating">Radiocarbon dating</a> is used for geologically young materials containing <a href="/wiki/Organic_matter" title="Organic matter">organic carbon</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Nance&#95;Murphy&#95;2016&#95;54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nance_Murphy_2016-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Thermochemical techniques can be used to determine temperature profiles within the crust, the uplift of mountain ranges, and paleo-topography.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Writing_better_articles#Stay_on_topic" title="Wikipedia:Writing better articles"><span title="The material near this tag may contain information that is not relevant to the article&#39;s main topic. (October 2025)">relevant?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Geological_development_of_an_area">Geological development of an area</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Geological development of an area"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Volcanosed.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Volcanosed.svg/500px-Volcanosed.svg.png" decoding="async" width="340" height="191" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Volcanosed.svg/960px-Volcanosed.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="288" /></a><figcaption>An originally horizontal sequence of sedimentary rocks (in shades of tan) are affected by <a href="/wiki/Igneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Igneous">igneous</a> activity. Deep below the surface is a <a href="/wiki/Magma_chamber" title="Magma chamber">magma chamber</a> and large associated igneous bodies. The magma chamber feeds the <a href="/wiki/Volcano" title="Volcano">volcano</a>, and sends offshoots of <a href="/wiki/Magma" title="Magma">magma</a> that will later crystallize into dikes and sills. Magma advances upwards to form <a href="/wiki/Intrusive_rock" title="Intrusive rock">intrusive igneous bodies</a>. The diagram illustrates both a <a href="/wiki/Cinder_cone" title="Cinder cone">cinder cone</a> volcano, which releases ash, and a <a href="/wiki/Composite_volcano" class="mw-redirect" title="Composite volcano">composite volcano</a>, which releases both lava and ash.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fault_types.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Fault_types.svg/250px-Fault_types.svg.png" decoding="async" width="230" height="460" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Fault_types.svg/500px-Fault_types.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="1024" /></a><figcaption> An illustration of the three types of faults.<br /> A. Strike-slip faults occur when rock units slide past one another.<br /> B. Normal faults occur when rocks are undergoing horizontal extension.<br /> C. Reverse (or thrust) faults occur when rocks are undergoing horizontal shortening.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Andreas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8d/San_Andreas.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="257" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="338" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/San_Andreas_Fault" title="San Andreas Fault">San Andreas Fault</a> in <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a> is a strike-slip fault</figcaption></figure>
<p>The geology of an area changes through time as rock units are deposited and inserted, and deformational processes alter their shapes and locations.
</p><p>Rock units are first emplaced either by deposition onto the surface or intrusion into the <a href="/wiki/Country_rock_(geology)" title="Country rock (geology)">overlying rock</a>. Deposition can occur when sediments settle onto the surface of the Earth and later <a href="/wiki/Lithification" title="Lithification">lithify</a> into sedimentary rock,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or when as <a href="/wiki/Volcanic_rock" title="Volcanic rock">volcanic material</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Volcanic_ash" title="Volcanic ash">volcanic ash</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or <a href="/wiki/Lava_flow" class="mw-redirect" title="Lava flow">lava flows</a> blanket the surface. <a href="/wiki/Igneous_intrusion" title="Igneous intrusion">Igneous intrusions</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Batholith" title="Batholith">batholiths</a>, <a href="/wiki/Laccolith" title="Laccolith">laccoliths</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dike_(geology)" title="Dike (geology)">dikes</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sill_(geology)" title="Sill (geology)">sills</a>, push upwards into the overlying rock, and crystallize as they intrude.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hunt&#95;2005&#95;45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hunt_2005-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>After the initial sequence of rocks has been deposited, the rock units can be <a href="/wiki/Deformation_(mechanics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Deformation (mechanics)">deformed</a> and/or <a href="/wiki/Metamorphism" title="Metamorphism">metamorphosed</a>. Deformation typically occurs as a result of horizontal shortening, <a href="/wiki/Extension_(geology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Extension (geology)">horizontal extension</a>, or side-to-side (<a href="/wiki/Strike-slip" class="mw-redirect" title="Strike-slip">strike-slip</a>) motion. These structural regimes broadly relate to <a href="/wiki/Convergent_boundaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Convergent boundaries">convergent boundaries</a>, <a href="/wiki/Divergent_boundary" title="Divergent boundary">divergent boundaries</a>, and transform boundaries, respectively, between tectonic plates.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Twiss&#95;Moores&#95;1992&#95;63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Twiss_Moores_1992-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Pages: 7–9">&#58;&#8202;7–9&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>When rock units are placed under horizontal <a href="/wiki/Compression_(geology)" title="Compression (geology)">compression</a>, they shorten and become thicker. Because rock units, other than muds, <a href="/wiki/Incompressible_surface" title="Incompressible surface">do not significantly change in volume</a>, this is accomplished in two primary ways: through <a href="/wiki/Faulting" class="mw-redirect" title="Faulting">faulting</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fold_(geology)" title="Fold (geology)">folding</a>. In the shallow crust, where <a href="/wiki/Brittle_deformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Brittle deformation">brittle deformation</a> can occur, thrust faults form, which causes the deeper rock to move on top of the shallower rock.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Because deeper rock is often older, as noted by the <a href="/wiki/Law_of_superposition" title="Law of superposition">principle of superposition</a>, this can result in older rocks moving on top of younger ones.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Movement along faults can result in folding, either because the faults are not planar or because rock layers are dragged along, forming drag folds as slip occurs along the fault.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Twiss&#95;Moores&#95;1992&#95;63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Twiss_Moores_1992-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Pages: 57–58">&#58;&#8202;57–58&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Deeper in the Earth, rocks behave plastically and fold instead of faulting. These folds can either be those where the material in the center of the fold buckles upwards, creating "<a href="/wiki/Antiform" class="mw-redirect" title="Antiform">antiforms</a>", or where it buckles downwards, creating "<a href="/wiki/Synform" class="mw-redirect" title="Synform">synforms</a>". If the tops of the rock units within the folds remain pointing upwards, they are called <a href="/wiki/Anticline" title="Anticline">anticlines</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syncline" title="Syncline">synclines</a>, respectively. If some of the units in the fold are facing downward, the structure is called an overturned anticline or syncline, and if all of the rock units are overturned or the correct up-direction is unknown, they are simply called by the most general terms, antiforms, and synforms.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antecline_(PSF).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Antecline_%28PSF%29.png/250px-Antecline_%28PSF%29.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="135" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Antecline_%28PSF%29.png/500px-Antecline_%28PSF%29.png 1.5x" data-file-width="609" data-file-height="328" /></a><figcaption>A diagram of folds, indicating an <a href="/wiki/Anticline" title="Anticline">anticline</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Syncline" title="Syncline">syncline</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Even higher pressures and temperatures during horizontal shortening can cause both folding and <a href="/wiki/Metamorphism" title="Metamorphism">metamorphism</a> of the rocks. This metamorphism causes changes in the <a href="/wiki/Mineral" title="Mineral">mineral composition</a> of the rocks; creates a <a href="/wiki/Foliation_(geology)" title="Foliation (geology)">foliation</a>, or planar surface, that is related to mineral growth under stress. This can remove signs of the original textures of the rocks, such as <a href="/wiki/Bed_(geology)" title="Bed (geology)">bedding</a> in sedimentary rocks, flow features of <a href="/wiki/Lava" title="Lava">lavas</a>, and crystal patterns in <a href="/wiki/Crystalline_rock" class="mw-redirect" title="Crystalline rock">crystalline rocks</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Extension causes the rock units as a whole to become longer and thinner. This is primarily accomplished through <a href="/wiki/Normal_fault" class="mw-redirect" title="Normal fault">normal faulting</a> and through the ductile stretching and thinning. Normal faults drop rock units that are higher below those that are lower. This typically results in younger units ending up below older units. Stretching of units can result in their thinning.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In fact, at one location within the <a href="/wiki/Maria_Fold_and_Thrust_Belt" class="mw-redirect" title="Maria Fold and Thrust Belt">Maria Fold and Thrust Belt</a>, the entire sedimentary sequence of the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Canyon" title="Grand Canyon">Grand Canyon</a> appears over a length of less than a meter.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Rocks at the depth to be ductilely stretched are often metamorphosed. These stretched rocks can pinch into lenses, known as <i><a href="/wiki/Boudinage" title="Boudinage">boudins</a></i>, after the French word for "sausage" because of their visual similarity.
</p><p>Where rock units slide past one another, <a href="/wiki/Strike-slip_fault" class="mw-redirect" title="Strike-slip fault">strike-slip faults</a> develop in shallow regions, and become <a href="/wiki/Shear_zone" title="Shear zone">shear zones</a> at deeper depths where the rocks deform ductilely.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kittatinny_Mountain_Cross_Section.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/06/Kittatinny_Mountain_Cross_Section.jpg" decoding="async" width="350" height="241" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="432" data-file-height="298" /></a><figcaption>Geological <a href="/wiki/Cross_section_(geology)" title="Cross section (geology)">cross section</a> of <a href="/wiki/Kittatinny_Mountain" title="Kittatinny Mountain">Kittatinny Mountain</a>. This cross-section shows metamorphic rocks, overlain by younger sediments deposited after the metamorphic event. These rock units were later folded and faulted during the uplift of the mountain.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The addition of new rock units, both depositionally and intrusively, often occurs during deformation. Faulting and other deformational processes result in the creation of topographic gradients, causing material on the rock unit that is increasing in elevation to be eroded by hillslopes and channels. These sediments are deposited on the rock unit that is going down. Continual motion along the fault maintains the topographic gradient in spite of the movement of sediment and continues to create <a href="/wiki/Accommodation_space" class="mw-redirect" title="Accommodation space">accommodation space</a> for the material to deposit.
</p><p>Deformational events are often associated with volcanism and igneous activity.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Volcanic ashes and lavas accumulate on the surface, and igneous intrusions enter from below. <a href="/wiki/Dike_(geology)" title="Dike (geology)">Dikes</a>, long, planar igneous intrusions, enter along cracks, and therefore often form in large numbers in areas that are being actively deformed. This can result in the emplacement of <a href="/wiki/Dike_swarm" title="Dike swarm">dike swarms</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> such as those that are observable across the Canadian shield,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or rings of dikes around the <a href="/wiki/Lava_tube" title="Lava tube">lava tube</a> of a volcano.
</p><p>All of these processes do not necessarily occur in a single environment and do not necessarily occur in a single order. The <a href="/wiki/Hawaiian_Islands" title="Hawaiian Islands">Hawaiian Islands</a>, for example, consist almost entirely of layered <a href="/wiki/Basalt" title="Basalt">basaltic</a> lava flows. The sedimentary sequences of the mid-continental United States and the <a href="/wiki/Geology_of_the_Grand_Canyon_area" title="Geology of the Grand Canyon area">Grand Canyon</a> in the southwestern United States contain almost-undeformed stacks of sedimentary rocks that have remained in place since <a href="/wiki/Cambrian" title="Cambrian">Cambrian</a> time. Other areas are much more geologically complex. In the southwestern United States, sedimentary, volcanic, and intrusive rocks have been metamorphosed, faulted, foliated, and folded. Even older rocks, such as the <a href="/wiki/Acasta_gneiss" class="mw-redirect" title="Acasta gneiss">Acasta gneiss</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Slave_craton" class="mw-redirect" title="Slave craton">Slave craton</a> in northwestern <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Oldest_rock" class="mw-redirect" title="Oldest rock">oldest known rock in the world</a> have been metamorphosed to the point where their origin is indiscernible without laboratory analysis.
</p><p>These processes can occur in stages. In many places, the Grand Canyon in the southwestern United States being a very visible example, the lower rock units were metamorphosed and deformed, and then deformation ended and the upper, undeformed units were deposited. Although any amount of rock emplacement and rock deformation can occur, and they can occur any number of times, these concepts provide a guide to understanding the <a href="/wiki/Historical_geology" title="Historical geology">geological history</a> of an area.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Investigative_methods">Investigative methods</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Investigative methods"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Brunton.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Brunton.JPG/250px-Brunton.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Brunton.JPG/500px-Brunton.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="2593" /></a><figcaption>A standard <a href="/wiki/Brunton_compass" title="Brunton compass">Brunton Pocket Transit</a>, commonly used by geologists for mapping and surveying</figcaption></figure>
<p>Geologists use a number of fields, laboratory, and numerical modeling methods to decipher Earth history and to understand the processes that occur on and inside the Earth. In typical geological investigations, geologists use primary information related to <a href="/wiki/Petrology" title="Petrology">petrology</a> (the study of rocks), stratigraphy (the study of sedimentary layers), and structural geology (the study of positions of rock units and their deformation). In many cases, geologists study modern soils, <a href="/wiki/River" title="River">rivers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Landscape" title="Landscape">landscapes</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Glacier" title="Glacier">glaciers</a>; investigate past and current life and <a href="/wiki/Biogeochemical" class="mw-redirect" title="Biogeochemical">biogeochemical</a> pathways, and use <a href="/wiki/Geophysics" title="Geophysics">geophysical methods</a> to investigate the subsurface. Sub-specialities of geology may distinguish <i>endogenous</i> and <i>exogenous</i> geology.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Field_methods">Field methods</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Geology&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Field methods"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:USGS_1950s_mapping_field_camp.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/USGS_1950s_mapping_field_camp.jpg/250px-USGS_1950s_mapping_field_camp.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/USGS_1950s_mapping_field_camp.jpg/500px-USGS_1950s_mapping_field_camp.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2672" data-file-height="1661" /></a><figcaption>A typical <a href="/wiki/USGS" class="mw-redirect" title="USGS">USGS</a> field mapping camp in the 1950s</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:PDA_Mapping.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/PDA_Mapping.jpg/250px-PDA_Mapping.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="205" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a0/PDA_Mapping.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="302" data-file-height="248" /></a><figcaption>Today, <a href="/wiki/Handheld_computer" class="mw-redirect" title="Handheld computer">handheld computers</a> with <a href="/wiki/GPS" class="mw-redirect" title="GPS">GPS</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geographic_information_systems" class="mw-redirect" title="Geographic information systems">geographic information systems</a> software are often used in geological field work (<a href="/wiki/Digital_geological_mapping" class="mw-redirect" title="Digital geological mapping">digital geological mapping</a>).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Geological <a href="/wiki/Field_work" class="mw-redirect" title="Field work">field work</a> varies depending on the task at hand. Typical fieldwork could consist of:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Geological_map" title="Geological map">Geological mapping</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
<ul><li>Structural mapping: identifying the locations of major rock units and the faults and folds that led to their placement there.</li>
<li>Stratigraphic mapping: pinpointing the locations of <a href="/wiki/Sedimentary_facies" class="mw-redirect" title="Sedimentary facies">sedimentary facies</a> (<a href="/wiki/Lithology" title="Lithology">lithofacies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Biofacies" class="mw-redirect" title="Biofacies">biofacies</a>) or the mapping of <a href="/wiki/Isopach" class="mw-redirect" title="Isopach">isopachs</a> of equal thickness of sedimentary rock</li>
<li>Surficial mapping: recording the locations of soils and surficial deposits</li></ul></li>
<li>Surveying of topographic features
<ul><li>compilation of <a href="/wiki/Topographic_map" title="Topographic map">topographic maps</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li>Work to understand change across landscapes, including:
<ul><li>Patterns of <a href="/wiki/Erosion" title="Erosion">erosion</a> and <a href="/wiki/Deposition_(geology)" title="Deposition (geology)">deposition</a></li>
<li>River-channel change through <a href="/wiki/Meander" title="Meander">migration</a> and <a href="/wiki/Avulsion_(river)" title="Avulsion (river)">avulsion</a></li>
<li>Hillslope processes</li></ul></li></ul></li>
<li>Subsurface mapping through <a href="/wiki/Geophysical_survey" title="Geophysical survey">geophysical methods</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
<ul><li>These methods include:
<ul><li>Shallow <a href="/wiki/Seismic" class="mw-redirect" title="Seismic">seismic</a> surveys</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ground-penetrating_radar" title="Ground-penetrating radar">Ground-penetrating radar</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Aeromagnetic_survey" title="Aeromagnetic survey">Aeromagnetic surveys</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Electrical_resistivity_tomography" title="Electrical resistivity tomography">Electrical resistivity tomography</a></li></ul></li>
<li>They aid in:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Exploration_geophysics" title="Exploration geophysics">Hydrocarbon exploration</a></li>
<li>Finding <a href="/wiki/Groundwater" title="Groundwater">groundwater</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Archaeological_geophysics" class="mw-redirect" title="Archaeological geophysics">Locating buried archaeological artifacts</a></li></ul></li></ul></li>
<li>High-resolution stratigraphy
<ul><li>Measuring and describing <a href="/wiki/Stratigraphic_section" title="Stratigraphic section">stratigraphic sections</a> on the surface</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Well_drilling" title="Well drilling">Well drilling</a> and <a href="/wiki/Well_logging" title="Well logging">logging</a></li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Biogeochemistry" title="Biogeochemistry">Biogeochemistry</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geomicrobiology" title="Geomicrobiology">geomicrobiology</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
<ul><li>Collecting samples to:
<ul><li>determine <a href="/wiki/Biochemical_pathway" class="mw-redirect" title="Biochemical pathway">biochemical pathways</a></li>
<li>identify new <a href="/wiki/Species" title="Species">species</a> of organisms</li>
<li>identify new <a href="/wiki/Chemical_compound" title="Chemical compound">chemical compounds</a></li></ul></li>
<li>and to use these discoveries to:
<ul><li>understand early life on Earth and how it functioned and metabolized</li>
<li>find important compounds for use in pharmaceuticals</li></ul></li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Paleontology" title="Paleontology">Paleontology</a>: excavation of <a href="/wiki/Fossil" title="Fossil">fossil</a> material
<ul><li>For research into past life and <a href="/wiki/Evolution" title="Evolution">evolution</a></li>
<li>For <a href="/wiki/Museum" title="Museum">museums</a> and education</li></ul></li>
<li>Collection of samples for <a href="/wiki/Geochronology" title="Geochronology">geochronology</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thermochronology" title="Thermochronology">thermochronology</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Glaciology" title="Glaciology">Glaciology</a>: measurement of characteristics of glaciers and their motion<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li></ul>
 <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1273380762/mw-parser-output/.tmulti" /><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:342px;max-width:342px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:99px;max-width:99px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:129px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Leica_DMRX.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Leica_DMRX.jpg/120px-Leica_DMRX.jpg" decoding="async" width="97" height="129"
